## Document 004: 115年8月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-09-09)

Report source: MOF
Date: 2026-09-09
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

115 年 8 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 9.4%，較上年
同月增 41.0%(按新臺幣計算增 52.6%)；累計 1 至 8 月出口 5,743.4
億美元，較上年同期增 44.2%(按新臺幣計算增 46.5%)。 
（二）進口 
8 月進口 601.0 億美元，為歷年單月第 3 高，較上月增 3.4%，較上
年同月增 44.3%(按新臺幣計算增 56.2%)；累 計1 至 8 月進口 4,374.3
億美元，較上年同期增 40.4%(按新臺幣計算增 42.4%)。 
（三）出入超 
8 月出超 223.0 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 55.2 億美元；
累計 1 至 8 月出超 1,369.1 億美元，較上年同期增 500.5 億美元。

### Page 2

2 
二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
受惠於人工智慧(AI)、 高效能運算及雲端服務等應用持續擴增，相關
產品出貨維持強勁， 加上價格攀升效 應，8月出口較上年同月增 239.7
億美元(+41.0%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 電子零組件出口 322.2 億美元(歷年單月新高)， 較上年同月增118.3
億美元(+58.0%)，其中積體電路增 115.5 億美元(+60.0%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+29.9%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.7 億美元(+24.9%)。 
2. 資通與視聽產品出口 331.0 億美元，較上年同月增 97.6 億美元
(+41.8%)，其中電腦及其附屬單元增68.7 億美元(+40.4%)、儲存媒
體增 19.1 億美元(+2.4 倍)、 交換器及路由器增9.7 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 礦產品出口 20.0 億美元，較上年同月增 8.3 億美元(+70.2%)，其
中石油煉製品增 7.8 億美元(+74.3%)。 
4. 基本金屬及其製品出口 29.6 億美元，較上年同月增 5.4 億美元
(+22.4%)，其中銅及其製品增 6.1 億美元(+1.5 倍)，鋼鐵及其製品
減 1.1 億美元(-8.7%)。 
5. 機械出口 26.8 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+15.2%)，其中
生產半導體等機械增 1.9 億美元(+42.1%)、未列名具特殊功能機械
增 0.4 億美元(+39.0%)。

累計 1 至 8 月出口較上年同期增 1,759.8 億美元(+44.2%)，以資通與
視聽產品增 1,023.7 億美元(+68.5%)、電子零組件增 629.5 億美元
(+45.8%)增加較多。

生產等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求之帶動，積體電路及資通產
品進口維持強勁，加上輸入物價明顯上漲，8 月總進口較上年同月增
184.5 億美元(+44.3%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口408.4 億美元， 較上年同月增129.4億美元(+46.4%)，
其中電子零組件增 82.5 億美元(+62.6%)、礦產品增 16.9 億美元
(+38.4%)、電腦之零附件增 14.6 億美元(+84.4%)、基本金屬增 4.5
億美元(+21.8%)、塑化原料增 4.0 億美元(+16.2%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 130.6 億美元， 較上年同月增 38.4 億美元(+41.6%)，
其中資通設備增 27.2 億美元(+1.2 倍)、半導體設備增 2.8 億美元
(+7.3%)、交通運輸設備增 2.8 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 消費品進口 55.7 億美元，較上年同月增 16.0 億美元(+40.5%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增12.1 億美元(+3.7 倍)、藥粧用品增 1.2
億美元(+17.4%)、手機增 1.1 億美元(+78.1%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月對陸港、日本出口創新高，對美國、東協為單月第 3 高 
1. 對美國出口 236.7 億美元，較上年同月增 40.6 億美元(+20.7%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 23.0 億美元(+14.6%)、電子零組件增 11.6
億美元(+1.5 倍)、電機產品增 1.7 億美元(+30.1%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 211.5 億美元，較上年同月增 59.7 億美元
(+39.3%)，其中電子零組件增 47.4 億美元(+45.6%)、資通與視聽
產品增 6.7 億美元(+42.9%)、 基本金屬及其製品增4.6 億美元(+1.2
倍)。 
3. 對東協出口 151.3 億美元，較上年同月增 44.9 億美元(+42.2%)，
其中電子零組件增 25.3 億美元(+50.3%)、資通與視聽產品增 12.6
億美元(+41.1%)、礦產品增 4.6 億美元(+1.5 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 44.5 億美元，較上年同月增 11.5 億美元(+34.8%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 6.6 億美元(+57.0%)、電子零組件增 4.0 億美
元(+1.1 倍)，基本金屬及其製品減 1.4 億美元(-31.0%)。 
5. 對日本出口 36.6 億美元，較上年同月增8.9 億美元(+32.0%)，其中
電子零組件增 8.9 億美元(+76.7%)、 化學品增0.5 億美元(+32.3%)，
資通與視聽產品減 0.6 億美元(-13.9%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月對美國出口 1,803.2 億美元，較上年同期增 631.6 億美
元(+53.9%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,394.7 億美元，增 307.5 億美
元(+28.3%)， 對東協出口1,150.7 億美元 ， 增372.3 億美元(+47.8%)，
對歐洲出口 376.6 億美元 ， 增130.7 億美元(+53.1%)， 對日本出口247.0
億美元，增 54.7 億美元(+28.4%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月自中國大陸與香港進口 126.8 億美元 ， 較上年同月增47.6 億美元
(+60.2%)，自東協進口 106.9 億美元，增46.7 億美元(+77.6%)，自南
韓進口 91.7 億美元，增29.6 億美元(+47.7%)，自日本進口 57.0 億美
元，增 11.2 億美元(+24.4%)，自美國進口 53.9 億美元，增 14.3 億美
元(+35.9%)，自歐洲進口 49.5 億美元，增 6.2 億美元(+14.3%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增75.4%、
自南韓增 63.2%、自中國大陸與香港增 40.5%、自美國增 24.4%、自
日本增 24.1%、自歐洲增 12.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）今年 1-7 月主要國家/地區外貿景氣普遍上升，南韓(1-8 月)、香港、
新加坡出口動能尤為強勁，分別年增 52.8%、46.6%、38.3%；中國
大陸(1-8 月)、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-6 月)、日
本各年增 10.9%、8.1%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-7 月以香港進口年增 48.1%最
為顯著， 新加坡、 中國大陸(1-8 月)分別年增 37.0%、27.0%，南韓(1-8
月)、德國(1-6 月)各增 18.7%、11.7%，日本、美國各增 6.5%、1.2%。

圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）得益於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及 雲端服務等應用 需求強勁， 加
以外銷產品價格攀升，8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，年
增 41.0%，連續 34 個月正成長；進口 601.0 億美元，年增 44.3%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求擴張 ，及農工原料行情上
漲；出、進口互抵後，出超 223.0 億美元，亦創單月新高 。累計 1
至 8 月出口年增 44.2%、進口增 40.4%。
（二）8 月出口主要貨品以電子零組件、資通與視聽產品貢獻最多，年增率
分別為 58.0%及 41.8%，前者受惠 AI 商機及記憶體外銷價量齊揚，規
模值創單月新高，兩類併計增 49.3%，其餘貨類平均增 16.3%，以礦
產品隨油品售價上漲，年增 70.2%較為突出，基本金屬及其製品、電
機產品各增 22.4%、22.3%。累計 1 至 8 月資通與視聽產品出口值已
超越歷年水準，與電子零組件合計占總出口比重達 78.7%。

（三）8 月對主要市場出口同呈雙位數成長，其中對中國大陸與香港、日本
受電子零組件外銷強勁推升，出口值同創單月新高，各年增 39.3%、
32.0%，對東協、歐洲、美國分別增 42.2%、34.8%、20.7%；五大市
場之外，對墨西哥及澳大利亞出口皆增逾 2 倍。累計 1 至 8 月對美
國、歐洲出口各年增 53.9%、53.1%，對東協年增 47.8%，對日本、
陸港各增 28.4%、28.3%，對五大市場出口規模均為歷年同期最佳。
（四）展望未來，地緣政治衝突 及美國關稅政策變化等不確定 因素，持續
干擾全球經濟成長力道，惟隨人工智慧應用 不斷深化，算力需求攀
升，國際雲端服務業者加速建置 AI 基礎設施，加以國內半導體及伺
服器等供應鏈具競爭優勢，均有助支撐我國外銷動能，下半年出口
可望維持成長態勢。

## Document 008: 15.49 万亿！贝莱德资产管理规模已达美国GDP的一半

Source PDF: all report pdf/15.49 万亿！贝莱德资产管理规模已达美国GDP的一半.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

⻉莱德第三季度净流⼊ 2050 亿美元，营收同⽐增⻓ 19% ⾄ 77.5 亿美元。旗舰 iShares ETF
吸⾦逾 1350 亿美元，私募信贷净流⼊环⽐增加 50 亿美元。 CEO Larry Fink 表⽰正以加速势
头进⼊第四季度，私募市场及退休基⾦业务储备充⾜。但穆迪警告，⾼利率环境下私募信
贷市场⻛险仍在积聚。
摘要：
⻉莱德第三季度资产管理规模突破 15 万亿美元，刷新纪录，强劲的净流⼊与多元业务布局推动
营收同⽐⼤涨 19% ，私募信贷业务亦展现出相对韧性。
10 ⽉ 14 ⽇美股盘前，⻉莱德公布第三季度财报，公司资产管理规模达 15.49 万亿美元，刷新历史
纪录，这⼀惊⼈的数字，已相当于美国全年名义 GDP （约  31 万亿⾄ 32.5 万亿美元）的⼀半。
⻉莱德当季净流⼊规模达 2050 亿美元。营收同⽐增⻓ 19% ⾄ 77.5 亿美元， GAAP 每股收益为
10.57 美元，调整后每股收益为 15.93 美元。
财报公布当⽇，⻉莱德股价⾛出了明显的 “ 冲⾼回落 ” ⾏情，周三早盘⼀度⼤涨  5% ，但随后涨幅

全数收窄，最终平盘收官。不过，买盘在次⽇周四重新发⼒，推动股价强势反弹，收盘⼤涨超
4% 。
净流⼊创纪录， CEO 强调进⼊最强季度
⻉莱德董事⻓兼⾸席执⾏官 Larry Fink 在财报中表⽰，客⼾本季度委托管理的净流⼊规模达 2050
亿美元，过去⼀年累计净流⼊则⾼达 8690 亿美元。他指出：
旗舰 iShares ETF 产品线本季度净吸⾦近 1350 亿美元，成为流⼊的重要来源。公司同期还回购了
5.5 亿美元股票。 Fink 在声明中表⽰：
我们正以加速的势头进⼊季节性最强的第四季度。

展望第四季度，他表⽰公司在私募市场、退休基⾦等多个业务条线均储备了充⾜的项⽬管道。
此外，⻉莱德旗下⼈⼯智能基础设施合作项⽬在本季度完成了⾸笔部署，过去⼗⼆个⽉私募市
场已累计募资 680 亿美元。
私募信贷赎回压⼒边际缓解，但⾏业⻛险仍存
今年以来，私募信贷⾏业整体承压，市场对软件⾏业贷款敞⼝的担忧及⼤规模赎回请求持续困
扰该板块。⻉莱德的私募信贷业务表现出相对抗压性，净流⼊环⽐增加 50 亿美元。
⻉莱德⾸席财务官 Martin Small 在分析师电话会议中表⽰，私募信贷的投资需求整体稳健。他补
充称：
赎回压⼒⽅⾯，⻉莱德企业贷款基⾦的赎回申请占⽐从第⼆季度的 13.3% 降⾄最新季度的约
11.5% ，边际有所缓解。
不过，评级机构穆迪在 10 ⽉ 14 ⽇的报告中警告称，实际情况可能⽐已披露的违约率更为严峻。

穆迪指出：
市场基本⾯强劲，受新技术驱动，企业利润率与盈利动能均持续改善。
我们认为，在整个平台上提供私⼈⾼评级信贷存在更⼤、更持久的机遇，同时也有助于深化客⼾
关系。
在较⾼利率持续维持、再融资困难、赎回压⼒以及透明度不⾜等因素的共同拖累下，美国私募信
贷市场中较弱的中间市场借款⼈资产质量⻛险正在积聚。

市場焦點
美國
• 9月ISM服務業PMI降至54.9，雖商業活動與新訂單放緩 ，但就業回升至 50.1、訂單積壓增加 ，
顯示服務需求仍具韌性；另一方面，價格指數升至74.0，創2022年7月以來新高，供應商交貨
速度亦放緩 ，反映燃料 、原物料及供應鏈成本升高 ，景氣未明顯轉弱之際 ，通膨壓力反而重
新升溫；同時全球長債殖利率同步走高 ，AI基建投資延續企業成長動能 ，也提高資本需求 。
整體而言 ，美國經濟尚未明顯轉弱 ，但通膨與長端利率壓力同步升高 ，使金融條件維持偏緊 。
中國
• 彭博指出在政府積極推動開源策略與 AI普及下，中國產業快速發展出現供給過剩 ，產業獲利
能力面臨挑戰，未來三年中國AI產業將可能處於虧損狀態，不過即使模型價格下降，AI使用量
增加將帶動Token需求大幅成長，其中具有企業客戶、專有資料及生態優勢的AI龍頭，將比單

純提供模型服務的公司更具長期競爭力。
黃金
• 歐美國家財政可持續性的疑慮持續發酵 ，加上長期通膨風險升溫 ，宏觀不確定性讓實體黃金
市場逆勢吸金 。本波下跌主要反映去槓桿交易 ，實體黃金的基本面未變 ，有望為金價帶來中
期的支撐。建議投資人逢修正分批進場 ，可透過黃金存摺或是實體黃金 ETF(GLD)，以投資組
合多元化的角度布局。波段投機者，從技術指標來看，可留意4,000美元關卡是否出現支撐。

美國9月ISM服務需求略降、成本壓力升高，景氣韌性與通膨風險並存
• 服務業維持擴張，需求略降但就業回穩：9月ISM服務業指數降至54.9(前月55.4)，連續27個月位於擴張區間；商業活動
指數降至56.5、新訂單降至 59.8，顯示服務需求較前月放緩 ，但就業指數回升至 50.1、重返擴張區間 ，訂單積壓升至
56.6，顯示內需仍具韌性，服務業尚未出現明顯轉弱訊號。
• 成本與供應鏈壓力升高 ，通膨降溫面臨干擾： 價格指數升至 74.0，創2022年7月以來新高 ，且為7個月來第6次高於70；
供應商交貨指數升至53.2，顯示交貨速度放緩，燃料、柴油、鋼材、銅及記憶體等成本上升，加上供應受限，使服務業
投入成本壓力擴大，通膨降溫進程面臨干擾。
• 市場觀察：美國服務業為經濟成長的重要支撐，目前經濟呈現「需求仍強、通膨升溫」的拉鋸格局，雖服務業景氣支撐

美國企業獲利，但成本上升可能推升利率波動，投資上宜偏向美國短中天期投資級債，兼顧較高收益率與利率風險控管；
股票則可聚焦具定價能力、獲利與現金流穩健的優質企業。
資料來源：Bloomberg， 2026.10.6 資料來源：ISM Services PMI Report， 2026.10.6

全球利率同步走高、AI投資持續擴張，長端美債壓力仍高
• 全球長債殖利率同步走高，反映利率環境重新定價：美國財政部長貝森特指出，近期美債殖利率攀升與德國、日本等主
要債市同步上行，反映全球資本成本重新定價，而非市場對美國資產失去信心；加上目前尚未出現資金大規模撤離美債
並轉向其他主權債券的現象，因此債市波動較偏向全球利率環境調整，而非金融系統性風險。
• AI投資增加資金需求，支撐較高利率環境：貝森特亦駁斥AI泡沫論，認為微軟、Alphabet及Meta等大型科技企業仍以
實際獲利與現金流支撐持續擴大AI資本支出，不同於過去缺乏基本面的科技泡沫；龐大的資料中心 、電力與半導體投資
需求提高整體資金成本，也成為推升長天期利率的重要因素之一。
• 市場觀察：目前市場經濟近況屬於利率高檔、成長尚穩的情境中，因而投資策略建議配置大型科技股、AI基建供應鏈及

受惠利差環境的金融股具相對優勢；債券配置則以中短天期投資級債優先，避免長天期債券受利率上行壓力侵蝕。

• 美國8月貿易逆差擴大至2025年初以來最高水平：美國提高進口關稅後，貿易失衡問題仍未明顯改善，商務部數據顯示，8
月貿易逆差擴大13.7%至1,056億美元，且超出市場預期的1,021億美元。經通膨調整後，8月商品貿易逆差擴大至1,147億美
元，同樣為2025年3月以來最大。
• 觀察美國本土投資能否進一步轉化為出口能力：AI建設帶動半導體與設備進口，加上能源成本上升及關稅政策干擾，推升進
口額至歷史新高。不過，強勁內需也顯示消費與企業投資仍具支撐力。隨著第三季財報季即將展開，根據Factset數據，分析
師預期第三季標普500企業獲利年增約29.5%，加上企業獲利預期持續上修，強化投資人對高利率環境下企業基本面的信心。
投資策略上，相較於單筆投資，此時的高成長與高波動的環境，更適合分批布局的定期定額策略來參與市場。

• 實質薪資持續成長，內需復甦動能增強：日本8月實質現金收入年增1.5%，連續第8個月成長，名目工資與基本工資均年增
3.8%，顯示薪資成長已逐步抵銷通膨對購買力的侵蝕；剔除獎金、加班費及抽樣偏差後，全職勞工薪資仍成長2.8%，反映
薪資上漲具有一定持續性。實質所得改善有助家庭消費與內需回升，進一步支撐企業營收與獲利展望。
• 薪資與通膨並行，日股中長期仍具配置價值：薪資持續上升有助日本形成薪資增加、進而消費擴大，導向企業營收改善的正
向循環，同時也強化日本央行持續推進貨幣政策正常化的條件。雖然升息可能推升日圓與資金成本，但金融股可受惠於利差
擴大，且企業治理改革、AI投資及自動化需求仍提供獲利支撐。近期日股AI與半導體族群已明顯走強，顯示市場仍重視企業
獲利成長；中長期建議維持日股正向看法，聚焦金融、AI、機器人、自動化及電力設備等具結構性成長題材的企業。

中國AI產業陷入逐底競爭困境
• 中國AI產業陷入逐底競爭困境 ：彭博指出在政府積極推動開源策略與
AI普及下，中國AI產業快速發展 ，但也逐漸出現供給過剩 ，使產業獲
利能力面臨挑戰 。開源模式雖有助於降低技術門檻 、應用普及 ，但也
使技術優勢難以長期維持 ，加上中國市場長期習慣低價甚至免費的數
位服務，多數AI業者難以透過訂閱模式獲利，轉而依賴利潤較低的API
服務作為主要收入來源 。目前中國已備案大型語言模型 (LLM)數量達
988個，包括DeepSeek、Kimi及智譜AI等新創企業快速崛起，進一步
加劇市場競爭。此外API服務逐漸被視為基礎公用服務，即使AI Agent
帶動詞元(Token)需求快速成長，也未必能同步提升獲利，由於開源模
型可由不同雲端業者部署 ，市場進入門檻降低 ，業者更難建立長期競

爭優勢。
• 後市展望： 考量龐大資本支出與持續價格競爭 ，未來三年中國 AI產業
整體仍可能處於虧損狀態 ，市場將形成「美國閉源、高毛利」與「中
國開源 、低成本 」模式 ，即使模型價格下降 ，AI 使用量增加將帶動
Token需求大幅成長 ，進而推升資料中心 、雲端服務及 AI晶片需求 ，
從投資角度來看，基礎設施供應商(晶片、伺服器、資料中心、雲端)受
惠程度可能高於模型開發商；而具有企業客戶 、專有資料及生態優勢
的AI龍頭，將比單純提供模型服務的公司更具長期競爭力。
中國AI市場規模預測
中國已備案的LLM數量
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.10.6

人行下調抵押補充貸款利率和房貸貼息等政策出台，有望提振中國經濟
和市場信心
• 人行下調抵押補充貸款利率和房貸貼息等政策出台 ，有望提振中國經濟和市場信心 ：日前中國推出購房貸款貼息政策及下調
PSL(抵押補充貸款)利率，代表穩增長政策進一步加碼 ，有助於提振市場信心與經濟預期 ，此次政策不僅支持房地產市場 ，也
將「六張網」建設、科技創新、民營及小微企業 ，納入重點扶持範圍 ，透過增加再貸款額度 ，與提高支持比例 ，帶動投資、
就業及消費回升。短期A股有望在政策推動下出現築底反彈，其中基建投資、製造業投資、房市銷售及居民消費有機會改善，
同時PSL(抵押補充貸款)降息有助於流動性環境邊際寬鬆 ，降低市場對經濟增長的疑慮 ，後市來看企業獲利與信用環境有望逐
步修復，進一步鞏固A股慢牛格局。投資建議上房貸貼息政策有利房地產、建材、家居等產業鏈，金融業受惠資金成本下降與

信貸回升，食品飲料、零售、家電及服務消費則可望受惠於居民消費意願提升。
資料來源：華金證券，2026.10.5

中國、日本減持美國公債的背後邏輯與差異
• 橋水基金創始人達利歐警告 ，美國公債正面臨中日兩國減持的風險 ，數據顯示這項警告已成事實 。2026年前七個月，中國
美國公債持倉減少660億美元至6,180億美元；同期日本持倉亦減少820億美元至1.1兆美元，扣除價格變動的估值效應後，
兩國實質持倉均大幅下滑。然而，兩國削減美國公債的背後動機並不相同，這將導致未來的減持軌跡出現明顯分化。
• 中國戰略性長期去美元化：1.降低地緣政治風險與轉向資產多元化 ，隨著人民幣在國際貿易結算中使用增加 ，市場流動性提
升，中國對美元儲備依賴度顯著下降。2.美債財政可持續性隱憂，美國政府債務持續攀升，引發對美國長期財政健康疑慮。
3.根據彭博經濟研究模型預測，在漸進式減持情境下，中國的美元儲備(以美債與機構債為主)到2050年可能減少約50%。

• 中國搭配戰術性節奏調整 (短期緩和因素)：儘管長期趨勢向下 ，但目前中美利差顯著 (美中30年期公債利差超過 350個基點)，
在美債殖利率維持高位期間，過快減持意味著實現債券價格下跌的虧損，且可能加劇人民幣升值壓力。因此，中國短中期內
傾向採取「戰略性減持、戰術性放緩」的靈活步調。
美國公債持倉：中國 vs 日本
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.10.8，美國財政部
中國預計將減少美元儲備
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.10.8，中國央行、國際貨幣基金組織、彭博經濟研究。

日本受政策利率與本土資本回流驅動
• 與中國的結構性考量不同，日本的減持主要出自週期性因素與匯率干預需求。
• 日本央行貨幣政策轉鷹： 日本央行正加快升息腳步 。隨著日債殖利率上揚 (30年期日債殖利率升至約 4.2%)，美日利差持續
收窄，吸引日本國內機構資金回流本土主權債市場。
• 匯率干預政策：日本為防範日圓過度貶值，日本財務省傾向出售部分海外美債資產以籌集外匯干預資金。若日圓再次面臨壓
力，此類拋售干預可能持續。因此，隨著日本央行逐步走向政策正常化，日本對美債的持倉大概會延續下滑軌跡。
• 小結與投資建議：美國公債作為全球無風險資產的地位正面臨結構性挑戰，中國的「長期去美元化」與日本的「本土利率回
升資金回流」將形成雙重壓力，這意味著美債殖利率短期內難大幅下行，美國財政部發債成本居高不下，進而推高全球資本

的融資成本。投資債券配置建議採取「中短天期＋階梯式配置」策略，長天期美國公債(20年以上)受發債供給過剩及中日兩
國長期減持影響，價格波動較大。建議投資人適度降低長天期美國公債暴險，轉向防禦性較佳的中短天期美國公債或高品質
高評級公司債，以鎖定當前較高殖利率同時控管利率風險。
殖利率差：美元與人民幣 vs 美元與日圓
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.29

黃金市場資金流向分歧，實體投資有望支撐中長期金價表現
• 利率與匯率因素，壓抑金價近期表現：9月以來，美國公債殖利率飆升、美元指數走揚，由於黃金本身不孳息，資金傾向轉
往固定收益市場，疊加美元走強壓抑國際購買意願，金價自8月底高點至今已下修約11%。
• 9月資金流向分歧，實體投資強、期貨市場弱：根據世界黃金協會統計，9月在金價下跌之際，全球實體黃金ETF卻吸引大量
資金流入，流入約100億美元(67噸)。另一方面，COMEX黃金期貨市場投機部位大幅下降 ，避險基金與其他機構投資人的
多頭部位明顯減少，造成期貨市場賣壓增加，進一步推動金價下跌。
• 實體黃金市場逆勢吸金反應投資人的避險需求：歐美國家財政可持續性的疑慮持續發酵，加上美伊衝突未解及AI算力需求強
勁，長期通膨風險升溫，宏觀的不確定性讓實體黃金市場逆勢吸金。從數據上來看，本波下跌主要反映去槓桿交易，實體黃

金的基本面未變 ，有望為金價帶來中期的支撐 。建議投資人逢修正分批進場 ，可透過黃金存摺或是實體黃金 ETF(GLD)，以
投資組合多元化的角度布局。波段投機者，從技術指標來看，可留意4,000美元關卡是否出現支撐。
資料來源：世界黃金協會，2026.10.7。
實體黃金ETF淨流量 費氏係數顯示4,000美元附近或有轉折

公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。
11. 屬基金資訊觀測站ESG基金專區所列之基金，有關基金之ESG資訊，投資人應於申購前詳閱基金公開說明書或投資人須知所載之基金所有特色或目標等資訊。

市場焦點
美國
• 9月綜合PMI升至58.4，創2021年7月以來新高 ，製造業與服務業同步加速 ，新訂單及就業亦
明顯成長 ，顯示消費 、AI投資與國防支出支撐景氣動能 。不過，需求快速擴張也加劇供應鏈
瓶頸，企業投入價格升至 2022年10月以來高點，燃料、運輸及原物料成本上升 ，顯示景氣韌
性與通膨壓力同步升溫，增加Fed政策維持緊縮的壓力。
美國
期中選舉
• 目前民調顯示 ，民主黨可能取得兩院或眾議院多數 ，這將有助於縮減未來財政赤字預期 ，進
而帶動10年期美債殖利率下滑 ，對長天期美債形成支撐 。此外既有資料顯示 ，選後一年美股
平均報酬率達12.4%，主因為政治不確定性降低，市場焦點重新回到經濟成長、企業獲利及貨
幣政策等基本面因素，進而帶動投資信心提升。

越南
• 越南股市正式納入富時羅素新興市場指數 。估計此次納入最高可引導 60億美元的資金重新流
入越南。資產管理公司Vanguard也計畫在未來幾年內，將對越南的投資額提升至約 25億美元。
• 越南股市短期仍面臨逆風 ，除了能源通膨壓力居高不下恐拖累經濟成長 ，今年以來越南成為
美國最大貿易逆差國，加上美方企圖阻止越南成為中國商品流入的中轉國 ，美對越的301條款
調查為越南出口額帶來更多不確定性。

美國景氣強勢擴張，通膨與利率壓力再升
• 企業活動創逾五年新高 ，製造業與服務業同步加速擴張 ：S&P
Global美國9月綜合PMI初值升至58.4，創2021年7月以來新高，
且連續四個月加速成長；服務業 PMI升至58.7（59個月高點）、
製造業PMI升至57.0（52個月高點），產出亦同步加速 ，加上
訂單與就業成長皆達近年最快 ，顯示內需、AI投資及國防支出
共同支撐企業活動。S&P Global依PMI調查推估經濟年成長約
5%，Q3則上看4%，反映短期景氣動能明顯增強。
• 供給受限推高成本 ，通膨壓力再升溫： 需求快速擴張同時推高
供應鏈與產能壓力 ，企業未完成訂單升至 2022年5月以來高點 ，
供應商交貨延遲亦明顯惡化 ，反映產能不足 ，投入價格指數升

至2022年10月以來新高 ，企業主要反映燃料 、運輸、原物料
及薪資成本上升 ，顯示供給受限與能源成本可能持續向終端價
格傳導，增加通膨黏著風險並提高Fed維持緊縮政策的壓力。
• 後市觀察：數據公布後，10年期美債殖利率一度升至約 5.12%，
創2007年以來新高 ，市場對10月再升息的押注亦升溫 ，短線
需關注供應鏈壓力是否緩解 ，以及後續ISM、就業與物價數據
能否確認通膨再升溫趨勢 。投資策略建議關注利率敏感度較低
的高品質成長股或防禦型資產 ，可適度布局受惠強勁內需與 AI
／國防支出的美股大型科技與工業類股 ，並留意升息環境下金
融股的利差受益機會 ，整體建議維持分散配置並嚴控利率風險 。
資料來源：S&P Global PMI, Bureau of Economic Analysis via S&P Global Market
Intelligence， 2026.9.24

能源成本升高推升通膨，利率維持高檔壓力升溫
• 能源與運輸成本上升，增加通膨黏著風險：中東衝突與
俄羅斯煉油設施遭攻擊 ，使全球柴油供應持續偏緊 ，燃
料價格及運輸成本同步上升；由於柴油廣泛應用於運輸 、
農業與製造業，價格上漲將透過物流及生產成本向下游
傳導。目前全球柴油庫存偏低 、煉油產能受限，若供應
中斷延續，可能使能源價格維持高檔並延長通膨壓力。
• 公債與企業債供給增加 ，長端利率壓力升高：美國財政
赤字及政府融資需求偏高 ，加上大型科技公司持續為 AI
資料中心等基礎建設籌資 ，債券供給增加使市場承接壓
力升高；在通膨預期未完全穩定的情況下 ，投資人對長
天期債券要求較高的期限溢酬 ，進一步推升長端殖利率 ，
同時AI相關企業債近期亦出現利差擴大 ，反映市場對大

量發債及資本支出回收期的關注升高。
• 資產配置面臨成長與通膨雙重拉扯： 能源價格、企業融
資成本及長端殖利率若持續處於高檔 ，將提高企業資金
成本並壓縮高估值資產的評價空間；同時 ，利率與能源
價格波動也可能擴大股 、債市震盪。因此資產配置建議
降低長天期債券對利率變化的敏感度 ，提高對通膨與利
率波動的防禦能力 ，並以現金流穩定、具備定價能力之
優質標的為核心。
資料來源：Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis via FRED ， 2026.9.8

資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.9

美國期中選舉-目前民調顯示民主黨可能取得兩院或眾議院多數
• 目前民調顯示民主黨可能取得兩院或眾議院多數 ：最新民調顯示，民主黨
於11月美國期中選舉，取得國會多數席次的機率已領先共和黨，根據市場
情境分析，期中選舉不影響既有法案執行 ，但將左右未來兩年的財政預算 、
稅制改革 、政府支出及國會監督權限 。情境(1)若共和黨續掌國會 ，減稅
政策延長、產業回流、關稅及移民限制等議程有望加速推進 ，財政擴張力
道可能進一步增強 。情境(2)分裂國會 ，兩黨制衡將使重大法案推動難度
提高，新增減稅與大型財政刺激方案較難落地 。情境(3)民主黨全面掌控
國會，則可能提高對政府政策的監督力度 ，政策方向偏向提高稅負與監管 ，
壓抑企業獲利 ，使政策環境趨向平衡。

美國期中選舉-對於股債市的影響
• 對股市的影響：美國銀行分析1900至2025年共31次美國期中選舉，資料發現標普500在期中選舉前12個月平均報酬約為2.9%，
低於所有年度平均 8.9%。因為市場在選舉前往往受到政策不確定性 、政黨競爭及投資人觀望情緒影響 ，波動相對較大 。然而
此現象不代表選舉必然導致市場疲弱，因為當時的經濟環境、通膨、利率及企業獲利等因素同樣扮演重要角色。期中選舉後12
個月的股市表現通常較佳 。歷史數據顯示，選後一年美股平均報酬率達12.4%。主因為政治不確定性降低，市場焦點重新回到
經濟成長、企業獲利及貨幣政策等基本面因素，進而帶動投資信心提升。
• 對債市的影響：若民主黨取得國會主導權，市場普遍預期財政支出擴張幅度將較受節制 ，有助於縮減未來財政赤字預期，進而

帶動10年期美債殖利率下滑，對長天期美債形成支撐，若共和黨持續全面掌控國會，市場可能預期減稅及財政擴張政策延續，
進一步推升財政赤字預估，帶動公債殖利率走高，對長天期美債形成壓力，不過市場已部分反映相關預期，實際衝擊幅度有限。
美國期中選舉前後對於美股的影響
資料來源：美國銀行，2026.7
資料來源：復華投信，2026.9

AI建設熱潮吞噬自由現金流
• 資本支出創新高：雲端服務巨頭(包括亞馬遜、Alphabet、微軟、Meta、甲骨文以及SpaceX等)正在全力擴充AI算力與資料
中心，2026年大型雲端服務巨頭的資本支出合計預估將接近 9,600億美元，而2025至2031年的累計AI資本支出更可能逼近
9兆美元。
• 自由現金流出現赤字： 高額的投資加上股東回報(股息與庫藏股買回)，使許多巨頭的自由現金流(FCF)轉為負值(如Alphabet、
亞馬遜、微軟、Meta及甲骨文等)，預計到2027年，部分企業將更加依賴外部融資，而非單靠內部現金生成。
• 債券發行量大增： 為了填補資金缺口 ，科技業債券發行規模急遽上升 ，2026年迄今，各幣別的投資級科技債券發行量已突
破4,000億美元，創下歷史新高。

獲利韌性與金庫深度：強勁EBITDA成為信用安全網
• 上兆美元的流動性護城河： 儘管債務增加，但科技十二巨頭合計持有近 1兆美元的等值現金，整體科技板塊流動性充裕 ，短
期內無流動性危機。
• EBITDA強勁成長消化債務：市場預期覆蓋的12家最大科技企業在2026與2027年的EBITDA合計將成長約35%，強勁的獲利
能力顯著降低槓桿比率的上升壓力，確保信評機構維護穩定的信用評級。
• 資本配置策略分化：(1)兼顧股東回報：蘋果、微軟、Meta與Alphabet在投入AI同時，仍維持大規模的庫藏股買回與股息發
放。(2)全數投入資本支出：亞馬遜將絕大部分現金導向資本支出(2026年資本支出預計達2,200億美元居首)，且不發放股息。
• 小結與投資建議：科技巨頭為維持AI技術與基礎設施領先優勢，短期內，負自由現金流將成為常態。然而，這並非財務危機

訊號，而是企業利用超強獲利能力 (高EBITDA)與龐大的現金儲備進行戰略性擴張 ，科技板塊的信用風險目前處於可控且健
康的狀態，投資建議聚焦AI變現能力與EBITDA成長，優先佈局具有強大獲利護城河且能將算力轉化為實質營收的龍頭企業 。

越南股市正式納入富時羅素新興市場指數
• 預估引導60億美元資金流入越南： 富時羅素估計，此次納入最高可引導 60億美元的資金重新流入越南 。受此提振，資產管
理公司Vanguard也計畫在未來幾年內，將對越南的投資額提升至約25億美元。整個指數過渡將分為四個階段推行至2027年：
今年9月率先納入10%，明年3月追加20%，最後於明年6月與9月分別完成剩餘各35%的分配。市場分析指出，隨著明年3月
配額加大，市場關注度與對本地投資人的影響將更為顯著。
• 越南股市短期仍面臨逆風： 儘管官方早前誓言今年 GDP成長目標達10%，但中東衝突以來能源通膨壓力居高不下恐拖累經
濟成長。此外，越南與美國的貿易協商仍在進行中，由於今年以來越南成為美國最大貿易逆差國，加上美方企圖阻止越南成

為中國商品流入的中轉國，美對越的301條款調查為越南出口額帶來更多不確定性。
• 觀點：儘管短線面臨不確定性 ，但越南股市同時具備多項利多題材 ，包含企業獲利成長、公共投資加速、FDI回流及資本市
場改革等。中長期結構仍偏正向，可以衛星配置的角度布局，或是以定期定額的方式參與長期成長潛力。

息日前一日之淨值×100%。

## Document 101: 8月海外资金重返美债市场，英国持仓首破万亿，中日继续减持、中国持债08年来新低

Source PDF: all report pdf/8月海外资金重返美债市场，英国持仓首破万亿，中日继续减持、中国持债08年来新低.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

据美财政部报告， 8 ⽉海外投资者净买⼊美债 1089 亿美元，持债规模扭转两⽉连降；⼗⼤
持债国家地区中， 8 ⽉只有中⽇持仓下滑，头号 “ 债主 ” ⽇本在⼲预汇市当⽉持仓创 22 个⽉新
低、连降四个⽉，中国持仓连降三个⽉，⼀年来减少 1090 亿美元，第⼆⼤ “ 债主 ” 英国持仓
两⽉连创历史新⾼，加拿⼤ 8 ⽉增持居⾸、增幅 383 亿美元，总持仓排第五，增幅第⼆位⽐
利时增 198 亿美元。
摘要：
美国官⽅数据显⽰，今年 8 ⽉，海外资⾦重返美国国债市场，但主要海外持有国家地区的配置⽅
向仍然分化，前三⼤ “ 债主 ” 中，⽇本和中国⼤陆继续减持，英国持仓续创新⾼。
美国财政部于美东时间 16 ⽇周五公布国际资本流动报告（ TIC ），披露 8 ⽉海外投资者净买⼊美国
国债 1089 亿美元，扭转 7 ⽉净卖出 36 亿美元的抛售势头。按持仓余额统计， 8 ⽉海外投资者持有
的美国国债总规模环⽐增加 833 亿美元，升⾄ 9.3317 万亿美元，在两个⽉连降后反弹。
美债的主要持有国家地区中，英国持仓规模史上⾸次突破 1 万亿美元，加拿⼤、⽐利时也⼤幅增

持。但海外资⾦重返美债市场，并不意味着所有主要持有国都在增加配置。⽇本的持仓⾃ 5 ⽉以
来持续下滑，和⽇本政府抛售美元资产买⼊⽇元的⼲预⾏动期重合，中国⼤陆则延续了⻓期减
持的趋势，⼀年来减少上千亿美元。
⽇本汇市⼲预规模创纪录，美元资产配置受关注
美国财政部 TIC 数据显⽰， 8 ⽉⽇本持有的美国国债规模环⽐ 7 ⽉减少 356 亿美元，降⾄ 1.0683 万
亿美元，仍是美国最⼤的海外 “ 债主 ” 。
据 TIC 报告，⽇本的美债持仓已连续四个⽉下降。此前 6 ⽉、 7 ⽉持仓连续创 2025 年 1 ⽉以来新
低， 8 ⽉进⼀步降⾄ 2024 年 12 ⽉以来最低⽔平。
⽇本持仓持续缩⽔之际，⽇本政府今年多次⼲预外汇市场、试图⽀撑⽇元，市场因此关注汇市
⼲预是否影响⽇本的美元资产配置。
⽇本财务省 5 ⽉ 29 ⽇公布的数据显⽰， 4 ⽉ 28 ⽇⾄ 5 ⽉ 27 ⽇期间，⽇本外汇市场⼲预总额达到
11.73 万亿⽇元。分析⼈⼠认为，⽇本买⼊⽇元、卖出美元的交易可能涉及⼀定规模的美国国债

出售。
此后，⽇美两国还罕⻅地联⼿⼲预汇市。⽇本财务省 8 ⽉ 3 ⽇证实，⽇本政府与美国财政部于 7 ⽉
31 ⽇协调实施了买⼊⽇元的汇率⼲预，以遏制⽇元汇率近期的剧烈波动。这是⽇美两国⾃ 2011
年东⽇本⼤地震后⾸次协调⼲预汇市，也是⾦融危机等特殊时期之外极为罕⻅的联合⾏动。
⽇本财务省随后公布的数据显⽰， 7 ⽉ 30 ⽇⾄ 8 ⽉ 26 ⽇期间，⽇本政府共计投⼊约 15.4 万亿⽇元
⼲预外汇市场，创下单⽉⼲预⾦额新⾼。
⼲预汇市通常需要卖出外汇、买⼊⽇元。由于⽇本外汇储备中持有⼤量美国国债，市场因此关
注，⽇本是否会通过出售美债等美元资产为⼲预⾏动提供资⾦。
不过， TIC 数据并不能直接证明⽇本为⼲预汇市出售了多少美债。持仓余额的变化既受到实际买
卖影响，也可能受到债券价格变化等估值因素影响，不能将⽇本 8 ⽉持仓减少 356 亿美元全部归
因于汇市⼲预。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

中国持仓连降三个⽉  两⽉连创⼗⼋年新低
据 TIC 报告，中国⼤陆 8 ⽉持有的美国国债规模环⽐减少 273 亿美元，降⾄ 5907 亿美元，连续两
个⽉创 2008 年 8 ⽉以来新低，继续位居美国第三⼤海外债主。
截⾄ 8 ⽉，中国⼤陆的美债持仓已连续三个⽉下降，最近 12 个⽉内，有⼗个⽉持仓环⽐下滑。以
TIC ⼀年来的数据计算，截⾄ 8 ⽉末，中国⼤陆所持美国国债相⽐去年同期⼤幅减少 1090 亿美
元。
从更⻓周期看，中国⼤陆持有的美国国债规模已较过去明显下降。 2016 年前后，中国⼤陆持仓
曾超过 1 万亿美元，如今已降⾄约 5900 亿美元，反映出官⽅持仓持续收缩的趋势。
与此同时，市场近期也出现关于中国可能通过第三地持有部分美国债券的讨论。据本周三的报
道，华尔街部分投资者和分析⼈⼠怀疑，中国可能通过加拿⼤等地的⾦融机构持有部分美国债
券。相关讨论与加拿⼤近年来美国国债及相关机构债券持仓增加有关。
但这⼀说法尚⽆确凿证据。加拿⼤持仓上升并不能直接证明其中包含中国转移的资产，也可能

受到其他投资者配置变化的影响。
因此，中国⼤陆持仓持续下滑是 TIC 数据能够确认的趋势，但仅凭公开持仓数据，尚不能断⾔中
国实际持有的全部美国债券规模同步下降。
加拿⼤增持居⾸，⽐利时持仓也⼤增
在 TIC 报告的⼗⼤海外持有美债国家地区中， 8 ⽉只有⽇本和中国的持仓减少。总持仓规模介于
中⽇之间的英国连续两个⽉增持。
数据显⽰，英国持有的美债规模环⽐增加 112 亿美元，升⾄ 1.0095 万亿美元，有纪录以来⾸次突
破 1 万亿美元，连续两个⽉持仓创最⾼纪录，继续稳居 TIC 报告的海外 “ 债主 ” 榜单次席。
加拿⼤和⽐利时的增持幅度更为突出。加拿⼤ 8 ⽉美债持仓环⽐增加 383 亿美元，升⾄约 4646 亿
美元，增幅在美国⼗⼤海外持有国中居⾸，总持仓排名第五。
⽐利时持仓环⽐增加 198 亿美元，升⾄约 4905 亿美元，增幅仅次于加拿⼤，总持仓排名第四。
值得注意的是，市场近期也在讨论第三地托管数据是否完整反映最终投资者的⾝份。美国财政
部的 TIC 数据主要依据托管机构和交易商报告编制，如果外国投资者通过第三国托管机构持有美

国证券，相关资产可能被记在托管地名下，⽽⾮最终所有者所在国家。
因此，单个国家持仓增加并不⼀定完全对应其本国投资者的实际买⼊。尤其在分析中国等⼤型
持有国的美债配置变化时，托管地与最终所有者之间的差异值得留意。
海外私⼈资⾦流⼊占主导，美债需求能否延续
整体来看，中⽇以外的主要持有美债地区 8 ⽉有不同程度的增加。
在美国⼗⼤海外美债持有国家地区中，总持仓第六位的开曼群岛持债⼩幅增加 29 亿美元⾄ 4630
亿美元，第七位的卢森堡持仓增 61 亿美元⾄ 4482 亿美元，第⼋位的法国持仓增加 150 亿美元、
增幅仅次于⽐利时，增⾄ 3634 亿美元，第九位的爱尔兰持仓增 19 亿美元⾄ 3521 亿美元，中国台
湾地区持仓增 23 亿美元⾄ 2984 亿美元，位居第⼗。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

## Document 144: A reprieve, not a recovery, for EU chemical producers

Source PDF: all report pdf/A reprieve, not a recovery, for EU chemical producers.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 13
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Iran war has hit EU chemicals less severely than feared,
with production, capacity utilisation and margins improving in
recent months. But renewed import competition will cloud the
outlook once Gulf oil and gas supplies to Asia recover. To reverse
the industry's long-term decline, producers and policymakers
must accelerate the green transition
Edse Dantuma
Polyethylene, the world's most common plastic, is used to make everyday
products like packaging ﬁlm, shopping bags, and bottles

Boost from softer Asian competition
The Iran war has hit European chemical producers less severely
than feared. After the ﬁrst tariﬀ shock in early 2025, demand
initially rose as customers brought forward purchases amid supply
chain uncertainty. The main boost, however, came from weaker
imports. Damage to Middle Eastern plants and Strait of Hormuz
blockades sharply reduced production and exports. About a quarter
of regional polyethylene capacity was damaged, with repairs likely

to take months.

### Page 2

Elsewhere in Asia, producers faced prolonged feedstock and energy
shortages. Asia depends far more on Gulf oil, gas and raw materials
than the EU does. Before the conﬂict, Asian countries sourced 40-
90% of their oil from the region, compared with around 20% for the
EU.
Earnings improve strongly, but production
returns to previous level
Reduced competition and tighter supply conditions drove three
consecutive months of growth in EU chemical production from

February to April, while sharp price rises provided a signiﬁcant
boost to second-quarter earnings. But the respite proved short-lived
as output contracted again as inventory restocking faded and
transport links through the Gulf improved.
By June, EU chemical production was 0.5% lower than a year
earlier and remained around 24% below early-2022 levels.
Meanwhile, exceptionally low Rhine water levels continue to disrupt
raw-material shipments and production schedules, particularly in

Germany and the Netherlands.

short-lived

### Page 3

*seasonally adjusted production, 2-month moving average
Source: Eurostat
Spain is Europe’s exception
Germany and the Netherlands have recorded the largest production
declines among the EU’s six biggest chemical producers. Together
with Belgium, they host Europe’s largest petrochemical and basic
chemicals cluster: the Antwerp-Rotterdam-Rhine-Ruhr Area. Highly
energy-intensive processes therefore weigh heavily on their
chemical sectors.
Spain stands out: chemical output has risen relatively strongly this

year and was the only top-six country to record growth in both
2024 and 2025. Its growth over the past decade, despite
ﬂuctuations, is also notable. Spain’s competitive position beneﬁts
from relatively low energy costs, a smaller share of basic chemicals,
a larger consumer chemicals segment, favourable domestic
demand and its location. A pipeline supplies Algerian gas, while EU
recovery funds have helped accelerate the expansion of renewable
power capacity.

Spain's chemical industry has held up
relatively well in recent years

### Page 4

*seasonally adjusted, 2-month moving average
Source: Eurostat
Temporary relief from competition and pressure on basic
chemicals
While the EU chemical industry has broadly been under pressure for
years, performance varies signiﬁcantly across segments. Four of
the ﬁve hardest-hit categories are in basic chemicals, which
produce base products and intermediates. Since early 2022, output
has fallen by between 25% in dyes and pigments and 47% in other
organic basic chemicals. The latter is particularly energy-intensive

and remains heavily reliant on fossil hydrocarbons for both energy
and feedstock. Compared with the previous downturn, basic
chemical producers beneﬁted from weaker international
competition in the ﬁrst half of this year. Only agricultural chemicals
fell sharply, as prolonged drought in the second quarter reduced
demand for crop protection products.
Consumer chemicals, including oils, extracts, perfumes and
toiletries, are the only segments to have grown both since early

2022 and since the start of this year. These downstream products
are less exposed to volatile energy prices and generate more value
through innovation. Speciality chemicals such as paints, coatings
and cleaning products sit between the two: output remains
structurally weak but has declined less than in basic chemicals.
Basic chemicals have been the hardest hit in
recent years

EU chemical activity follows the ebb and
ﬂow of the Iran war
The EU chemical production rebound was short-lived because of the
June US-Iran ceaseﬁre. Transport through the Strait of Hormuz
resumed and energy prices temporarily fell sharply. Chinese oil
reﬁning and exports of oil-based chemicals to the EU recovered. In
July, these exports were almost 40% higher than a year earlier,

while exports of organic basic chemicals – which account for about
two-thirds of China’s chemical exports to the EU – rose by nearly
50%.

chemical imports from China

### Page 6

Source: Eurostat
Production expectations rise as Middle East conﬂict ﬂares up
again
Since the conﬂict intensiﬁed again in July, Gulf oil, gas and
feedstock shipments crucial to Asian chemical activity have fallen
sharply. Although the switch from oil to coal oﬀers a partial
alternative, this is expected to increase pressure on Asian chemical
production and exports again, which will provide some relief to
European producers.
In August and September, most EU producers therefore expected to

raise output, with expectations reaching a two-year high in
September. European chemicals will beneﬁt from weaker
competition while transport remains disrupted. However,
alternative Gulf routes will probably gain importance if the conﬂict
persists, eventually reviving Asian import competition. Oil
shipments through Hormuz have recently increased to almost 80%
of pre-war levels.

### Page 7

Source: European Commission
Speciality and consumer chemicals have the
strongest outlook
Order books improved slightly again after a brief dip in September.
Yet producers’ assessment of orders remains much further below its
long-term average (-26% versus -14%) than production
expectations, highlighting persistent structural weakness.
The historically wide gap between upstream and downstream
activities has narrowed this year, as have sentiment diﬀerences

between countries. Sentiment improved in basic-chemicals-heavy
Germany, Belgium and the Netherlands, but weakened in France,
Italy and Spain, where basic chemicals play a smaller role. This
reﬂects basic chemicals’ temporarily improved competitive position
but also continued weak demand.
European growth remains constrained by higher inﬂation and only
a limited recovery in end markets. Higher energy prices squeeze
consumer purchasing power, hitting consumer chemicals hardest.

Speciality chemicals have better prospects as investment rises and
the AI boom lifts demand for products and materials used in:
Sustainability and electriﬁcation, including battery materials,
chemical catalysts, ﬂame retardants, lightweight resins and
composites.
Defence equipment, including reinforced plastic ﬁbres,
advanced coatings and lubricants.

### Page 8

Semiconductors, including photosensitive polymers and ultra-

Higher energy and feedstock costs will bite
more gradually
Chemical prices closely track energy costs, so expensive and
volatile energy will also make them higher and more volatile. Yet
the steepest rise in selling prices appears to be over. Although most
producers expected to raise prices in August and September, the
expectations index was stable just below its long-term average – a

far milder increase than after the Iran war began. This suggests
supply is gradually expanding again and price competition is
increasing.
Greater diﬃculty passing on input-cost increases will put margins
under growing pressure in the second half of the year, reinforced as
higher energy costs feed through into purchase prices. Layered
hedging – through long-term contracts, futures and other
instruments – spreads this impact over time and helped margins

improve sharply in the ﬁrst half. Large chemical companies in
particular use rolling hedging strategies, ﬁxing or mitigating prices
for diﬀerent periods: from several months to several years,
depending on the product, company circumstances and market
outlook.
Selling price expectations stayed benign in
September
Selling price expectations for the months ahead, EU chemical industry

### Page 9

Source: European Commission
Structural problems will resurface
The European chemical industry’s structural problems are far from
over. Demand has been weak for years, while competitiveness
continues to erode. European energy prices are substantially higher
than in the US and China, and government support is much lower
than in China. Once transport ﬂows normalise, global overcapacity
will again drive an inﬂux of cheap imports, especially from China.

Global chemical capacity grew by about 15% between 2018 and
2023 and, based on projects already underway, is expected to rise
by another 10% through 2028, keeping structural overcapacity in
place. Over the past decade, the EU’s share of global chemical sales
halved to 13%, while China’s nearly quintupled to 46%. The EU’s
chemicals trade deﬁcit with China widened from €21bn in 2023 to
€24bn in 2025. An artiﬁcially weak Chinese yuan and US import
tariﬀs further undermine Europe’s competitiveness.

Restructuring wave is not over
Despite somewhat better market conditions this year, restructuring
is likely to continue. Most major players have announced strategic
interventions in recent years. Earlier this year, SABIC sold its
European petrochemical operations, while Evonik is implementing a
multi-year plan of divestments, closures and reorganisations. Ineos
plans to close three UK chemical plants, and Shell wants to sell its
entire chemicals division, including a major European cluster.

European chemical capacity shrank by 30 million tonnes, or 7%,
between 2022 and 2026. Announced plant closures increased
sixfold over this period, while investment fell sharply. Petrochemical

Climate costs pose a growing challenge
Climate costs also put Europe at a competitive disadvantage to
countries without carbon pricing or with much lower pricing. As free

allowances are phased out, EU ETS costs will probably weigh
increasingly on production costs towards 2030, both directly
through companies’ own emissions and indirectly through higher
electricity prices. In July 2026, the European Commission proposed
extending the emissions-reduction pathway, modestly easing the
cost increase, alongside more ﬁnancial support for clean-
technology investment.
CBAM oﬀers only partial protection for EU chemicals
Fertilisers and hydrogen are covered by the CBAM levy, which

protects against imports not subject to carbon costs. In
return, free allowances for CBAM products will be phased out

faster, reaching zero by 2034. This will widen the cost gap
between eﬃcient, electriﬁed installations and older fossil-
based plants. Besides the faster phase-out, CBAM has several
drawbacks for chemical companies:
The levy can be circumvented when a chemical feedstock
is covered by CBAM but a product containing it is not.
This may encourage imports of ﬁnished or intermediate
products from outside the EU. The European
Commission’s proposed expansion mainly targets

downstream steel and aluminium products, not the
wider chemical value chain.
Most chemicals remain outside CBAM. Chemical value
chains involve many processes, intermediates and
feedstocks, making embedded emissions diﬃcult to
calculate. Organic chemicals have therefore been
excluded for now.
CBAM does not oﬀset the EU ETS-related competitive
disadvantage faced by EU companies in export markets.
Promising decarbonisation projects remain operationally

challenging
To regain competitiveness, European plants must decarbonise
faster through carbon capture and storage, process electriﬁcation,
and recycled and bio-based feedstocks. Although projects are
regularly cancelled, promising initiatives remain. INEOS plans to
start Project One in Antwerp this year, introducing a modern
ethylene cracker that initially emits less than half as much CO ₂  as
the EU’s most eﬃcient cracker and becomes climate-neutral within

ten years.
Spain’s Repsol aims to decarbonise its Tarragona complex through
circular chemicals and methanol, large-scale green hydrogen and
oﬀshore CO ₂  storage. In September, fertiliser producer Yara began
shipping liquid CO ₂  from Sluiskil in the Netherlands to Norway for
the permanent underground storage of 800,000 tonnes of CO ₂  a

Such projects can quickly cost hundreds of millions of euros a year.

The €5bn Project One has contributed to the doubling of INEOS’s
debt since 2021, while the group suﬀered a loss of more than
€500m in 2025. Chemicals are therefore widely seen as a hard-to-
abate sector. Governments often co-invest, but poor operating
conditions, inadequate green infrastructure and weak demand for
sustainable products still obstruct large-scale decarbonisation.
Infrastructure and decarbonisation
investment are essential
Through its European Chemicals Industry Action Plan, the European

Commission aims to prevent essential chemical production from
leaving the EU. The European Critical Chemicals Alliance brings
policymakers and producers together to identify, monitor, protect
and support critical European production. Industry supports the EU
ETS goals and system, but argues that suﬃcient free allowances
should remain until eﬀective protection against carbon leakage and
adequate demand for sustainable products are in place.
This requires supportive EU policy. Europe’s chemical

transformation is becoming a race against time: more plants will
inevitably close, leaving only the most eﬃcient and fastest-
decarbonising companies and clusters. Brussels’ growing focus on
incentives and safeguards against unwanted international
dependencies therefore oﬀers the sector welcome support.

## Document 200: Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-18)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-18).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
By Sian Fenner, Ryan Wells & Illiana Jain
13:33 October 02 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the
cash rate by 25bps to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given Governor Bullock had
already communicated that upside risks to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established by

the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.

### Page 2

Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks. The Middle East conflict was emphasised as
the risk that had “materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude oil and refined
product prices over recent months. The RBA’s liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of
costs and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic capacity pressures and the data
centre/AI investment boom were also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.

In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in
November is low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided there is no sudden resolution
of the Middle East conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the outlook for
energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying (trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr),
which was slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s forecast. As usual, the

monthly detail was mixed, but the key drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-related
components, particularly rents and new dwelling purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with
the unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel prices. We have maintained our forecast
for a 0.9% lift in trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building for Q4, a follow-up rate
hike in November appears most likely.

Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending surprised with a flat reading in August.
Services spend fell back following recent strength that was driven by major sporting events, but this
was offset by an increase in goods spend, particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending pulse is shading a touch softer. However,
another solid outcome for business investment could once again be on the cards for Q3. August’s

goods trade data showcased another significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our modelling suggests that the local
economic impact of such investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s latest update revealed that dwelling prices
fell another 1.1% nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have led the correction to

date, the fact that the other capitals are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest rates collide with the tax policy changes
announced in May’s budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced by the continued pull-
back in investor credit growth. Westpac anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For more, see our latest Housing Pulse.

Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength in household demand and higher energy
prices. Headline PCE inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased 0.2%mth and
3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%,
suggesting some easing in broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household spending

remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal
income growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling households opted to draw down savings.

### Page 3

The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the headline suggested. While the index edged down
0.1pt to 54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive months. There were some signs
of capacity constraints emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs up 4.6pts to 56.4,
suggesting that firms struggled to keep pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs increased. Firms responded by lifting

employment, 1.5pts to 52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed acceleration in cost
pressures, with the prices paid index jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every
major industry reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in

this week’s busy Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes, citing strong inflation,
diminished supply capacity and policy that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing to
progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time to assess upcoming data. The one thing
both agree on is that inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and extent of further rate
hikes. Our view remains that one further hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.

In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted support measures aimed at stabilising
growth and supporting the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its Pledged
Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and expanded quotas for targeted lending programs.
Authorities also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The subsidy applies to homes with
a floor area of 120m² or less and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of mortgage

costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of five years. The measures complement reforms
announced in August to improve developer financing conditions and support housing completion.
However, weak credit demand and confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy
transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement in activity, although the details were less
convincing. Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts to 50.1, supported by

stronger production and new orders. Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in prices,
with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market
conditions remained weak, with the employment index falling a further 0.6pts and remaining below 50
for almost four years. Conditions in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the services PMI
lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger construction activity. Input and output prices moved

higher, selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved to 48.5 and employment
increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some traction, but weak labour demand,
subdued credit growth and soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another constructive signal on the economy and

reinforced the case for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved by 2pts for large
manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while profitability indicators strengthened and capital
expenditure intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect fixed investment to increase by
11.6% in FY2026, while large non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%, pointing to

### Page 4

Related articles
continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with
measures of excess production capacity becoming less negative and indicating that demand remains
sufficiently strong to keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also intensified, with
employment conditions becoming more negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a

progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations remained anchored around 2%, providing
further evidence that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation environment. Overall, the
survey was consistent with an economy operating close to capacity and supports the view that a
December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

Earnings above all 
• Higher oil prices are in a tussle with stronger growth. We expect strong growth 
to help the global economy weather inflation risks, allowing equities to focus on the 
strength of earnings growth. Oil prices remain the key risk to monitor 
• Maintain a preference for equities over bonds and cash.  Strong earnings 
growth means we would use the recent pause to add exposure to our preferred 
markets and themes. The US and Asia ex-Japan remain our preferred regions 
• Take advantage of higher bond yields via credit risk but cap duration. The 
risk of higher bond yields looks most pressing in ultra -long maturities, but credit 
quality remains strong. We are Overweight EM USD bonds 
Hits from the 1990s 
Rising bond yields have been a key market theme.  Since mid -year, 10 -year 
government bond yields have risen approximately 40-80bps across the US, Japan and 
Euro area. Against this backdrop, gold has delivered high single -digit returns, while 
global equities have delivered low single-digit returns. 
Higher oil prices are in a tussle with robust growth data . The lack of progress 
towards a negotiated settlement in the US -Iran conflict and rising fears of shipping 
restrictions extending to the Bab al-Mandab Strait have meant that oil prices are once 
again testing the USD 100/bbl level. These worries have been exacerbated by 
increasing worries about refined product (eg. diesel, jet fuel, etc) supply. Despite these 
concerns, US growth data has held up remarkably well, as illustrated by the latest PMI 
and retail sales data. This is underpinned by AI investment growth.  
Real, rather than nominal, energy prices are key.  A chart of inflation -adjusted 
energy prices, though, illustrates why the global economy has managed to hold up 
despite these risks. Oil prices in real (inflation-adjusted) terms are closer to long term 
averages rather than at true ‘oil shock’ levels. This, together with the global economy’s 
lower oil intensity relative to history, is why we expect  growth and inflation to avoid 
worst-case scenarios as long as nominal oil prices avoid an excursion into the USD 
120-150/bbl range.

The Fed is likely to hike twice more by mid -2027. The 
acceleration in AI-driven investment growth means we believe 
the chances of a shift from our core soft-landing to no-landing 
economic scenario have gone up. This, combined with still -
high inflation readings mean we now expect the Fed to hike 
rates twice more by mid -2027. In contrast, the BoJ and ECB 
are expected to hike thrice and once more respectively. 
Buy-the-dip in equities 
1990s rate hikes a better parallel then 2022 . There is 
understandably some concern about whether rising central 
bank policy rates, and rising bond yields across maturities, will 
present a challenge to equity markets. However, we believe 
rate hikes in the 1990s are a better historical parallel, when 
central banks were hiking rates into strong economic and 
earnings growth, resulting in equity markets maintaining their 
pace of gains. The 2022 hiking cycle, in contrast, represented 
an inflation surprise, which we believe is not the case today. 
Strong earnings growth overwhelms other factors today. 
Still-strong economic and earnings growth means that we 
remain constructive on the outlook for equities and other risky 
assets going into year -end and beyond. The pause over the 
last couple of months was likely driven by a combination or 
rising bond yields , September seasonality and excessive 
investor optimism around mid-year.  
However, earnings expectations for global equities remain 
very strong (especially in our preferred US and Asia ex-Japan 
regional markets). We believe these earnings remain 
sufficiently robust to outweigh the drag from higher bond 
yields. Therefore, w e remain Overweight equities relative to 
bonds and cash, though we believe it is prudent to ensure the 
size of the equities Overweight is not excessive. Within Asia, 
we also trim China equities to a core holding, leaving Taiwan 
as our only regional Overwei ght, given the ongoing focus on 
AI investment-driven markets for now. 
Bumps remain part of the journey. As we laid out in our mid-
year outlook, the second half of this year is expected to be 
characterised by positive, but bumpy returns as a result of the 
factors laid out in the previous section, but also US mid -term 
election risks. The second half has thus far played out as 
expected given sufficient bumps, but still robust earnings 
growth. We would not be surprised by some volatility around 
US mid-term elections, but any further pullbacks or pauses 
would be opportunit ies to raise exposure given strong 
earnings growth is expected to ultimately dominate. 
Prefer credit over duration 
Corporate credit quality remains strong . Aggregate 
indicators of credit quality suggest credit quality remains 
strong across both Investment Grade and High Yield bonds 
across most regions. US IG bond yields face some upward 
pressure from significant hyperscaler supply, but supply -
driven spread widening tends to be relatively short -lived in 
nature and should not signal significant credit quality worries. 
Longer-maturity bonds remain at risk of further volatility. 
In contrast, we continue to be concerned that the volatility in 
very long maturity bonds could extend. Continued inflation 
and debt worries mean it is possible investors continue to 
demand a greater ‘term premium’ (ie. a premium for holding 
bonds with very  long maturities). We continue to believe 
limiting bond duration to 3-7 years remains appropriate.  
We continue to prefer  corporate and EM over government 
bonds. This implies earning a higher yield by taking on more 
credit risk (via corporate or EM bonds) rather than adding 
more duration risk. Within bonds, we remain Overweight EM 
USD bonds and view IG and HY bonds as core holdings. 
Modest USD weakness; gold to grind higher 
USD bounce led by pricing of Fed hikes . However, we 
believe Fed hikes are now excessively priced. At the same 
time, we see room for non-US rates to rise further, especially 
in Japan where the undervalued JPY is likely to strengthen. 
This is why modest USD weakness is likely to resume. 
Gold to continue grinding higher. While the move higher is 
likely to be characteri sed by two -steps-forward-one-step-
back, we believe an ultimately softer USD and continued EM 
central bank demand should result in continued gains in the 
precious metal over both the next 3- and 12-month periods.

2 Foundation: Our tactical asset allocation

### Page 7

Global Market Outlook 7

Summary

With the Federal Reserve’s latest decision to raise its benchmark rate by 25 basis points in September 2026, as well as the one 
to two additional hikes predicted  before the end of the year, we are entering a new interest -rate hiking cycle. This marks an 
important shift for investors, but higher policy rates do not necessarily imply weaker portfolio returns. What matters is why rates 
are rising, how quickly they increase, and whether economic growth can absorb tighter financial conditions. 
Our framework places the current environment in a healthy/high-yield regime. Growth remains resilient, credit spreads are 
tight and the yield curve is normalising. At the same time, the economy is approaching an important boundary. Inflation remains 
above target and the 10-year US government bond yield has reached 5%. A renewed rise in inflation or a sharper slowdown in 
activity could move markets into an unhealthy/high-yield regime. 
This argues for  measured risk-taking rather than an aggressively defensive stance . We maintain a slight overweight in 
equities, hold a strong overweight in gold and a neutral allocation to fixed income. Diversification remains key near a regim e 
boundary, and hedge funds can provide useful sources of return that are less dependent on the direction of traditional markets.

Not all interest-rate hiking cycles are the same

Traditional market 
analysis often treats 
rising rates as uniformly 
negative. History 
suggests a more 
nuanced conclusion.  
We classify hiking 
cycles along two 
dimensions: whether  
the increase in rates is 
healthy or unhealthy, 
and whether it occurs  
in a high- or low-yield 
environment. 
 Fig. 8 The different regimes of interest-rate hiking cycle 
Historical interest-rate hiking cycle defined by real growth and US 10-Year treasury yields

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered; Since 1974

A healthy increase in rates is generally associated with firm 
demand, above -trend growth, resilient equities and stable 
credit markets. Central banks can move gradually and remain 
data-dependent because the economy is strong enough to 
withstand tighter fin ancial conditions. An unhealthy increase 
is more likely to be driven by supply constraints or persistent 
inflationary pressures. Growth slows, credit spreads widen, 
equities de-rate and central banks are forced to react more 
aggressively. 
The second dimension is the starting level of yields. In a high-
yield environment, bonds offer meaningful income and a 
greater cushion against moderate price declines. In a low -
yield environment, that protection is limited. This distinction 
matters because the same increase in yields can have very 
different portfolio consequences depending on the income 
available at the outset.

Assessing today’s market regime: Walking the 
fine line  
We assess the current environment as healthy/high yield 
(see Fig. 9), although it is moving closer to the boundary with 
an unhealthy/high-yield regime. Growth remains resilient, with 
2026 consensus GDP growth of around 2.1%, but it is below 
its longer-term trend. Inflation has eased , but remains above 
target, with headline CPI at 3.4% year -on-year in August 
2026, leaving the Fed focused on the balance between 
persistent price pressures and a potential growth slowdown. 
Market signals over the next 6-12 months remain broadly 
supportive. The 10-year US government bond yield is around 
5%, providing the highest bond carry since 2007, while the 
yield curve is re -steepening after its prolonged inversion.  
Equities remain positive, supported by AI -related earnings 
and soft-landing expectations, and high -yield credit spreads 
are near post-global-financial-crisis lows. 
Overall, resilient markets and benign credit conditions support 
the healthy classification. However, slower growth and above-
target inflation leave little room for disappointment. Further 
disinflation would help sustain the current regime, while sticky 
inflation, a rapid rise in yields or a sharp growth slowdown 
could push it into unhealthy territory.  
Higher rates, different outcomes 
Historical returns vary significantly across the four regimes 
(see Fig. 10), but history shows that some hiking regimes 
have generated solid returns across major asset classes. 
Unhealthy regimes are more complicated. Unhealthy/high -
yield periods (top-left corner) have historically still generated 
positive average returns, but these averages can conceal 
considerable volatility and dispersion. The most damaging 
combination has been unhealthy/low yield (as in 2022), when 
traditional 60/40 portfolios declined by an average of 15.8%. 
The lesson is that the economic cause of higher rates, and the 
level of yields, matter more than the label "rate hike" alone.

Why equities can coexist with 5% bond yields 
The absolute level of bond yields does not, by itself, determine 
equity performance. Investors must also consider the 
direction, speed and underlying cause of the move. 
When yields rise gradually because growth and 
investment demand are strong, corporate earnings 
growth can offset pressure on valuation multiples.  By 
contrast, a rapid increase driven by an inflation shock or 
disorderly repricing is more difficult for equities to absorb. 
Historically, several high -yield episodes combined positive 
real growth with resilient equity markets, while inflation-driven 
episodes, such as in 2022, were more challenging. 
The current level of yields has also been substantially 
incorporated into market prices. Investors have had time to 
adjust discount rates, equity valuations and financing 
assumptions to a world in which the 10-year Treasury yield is 
around 5%. This does not eliminate risk, but it means high 
yields are no longer a wholly new shock. The greater  threat 
would be a further rapid and unexpected increase in yields. 
AI investment sits at the centre of today’s market dynamic. 
Heavy spending on data centres, semiconductors, power 
generation and digital infrastructure is supporting earnings 
and helping equities remain resilient, despite elevated yields. 
At the same time, AI-related capital demand is competing with 
rising government borrowing and bond issuance. If 
productivity gains and earnings growth ultimately justify the 
investment, higher yields and stronger equities can coex ist.

### Page 9

Global Market Outlook 9

However, if bond supply outpaces available savings or AI 
returns disappoint, yields could rise more sharply and put 
pressure on valuations. The balance between productive 
investment demand and expanding sovereign debt supply 
remains a key market theme. 
Translating the hiking-cycle regimes and 
yields level into portfolio positioning 
Our positioning reflects a healthy/high -yield regime, while 
recognising we are close to the boundary with a less 
favourable environment. Historically, this regime has 
supported positive returns across equities, bonds, gold and 
60/40 portfolios. However, pe rformance has varied 
considerably depending on whether yields were rising, stable 
or falling (see Fig. 11), reinforcing the case for selective risk -
taking and broad diversification. 
Fig. 11 Asset average returns under different high-yield 
scenarios 
Historical average performance of major asset classes 
across different path of bond yields.

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
We maintain a slight overweight in equities, focused on US 
AI-related stocks and Asia ex -Japan. Equities performed 
particularly well when high yields were stable, historically 
returning 15.1% on average, but struggled when yields rose. 
Our moderate overweight , therefore, allows participation in 
resilient growth and earnings while limiting exposure to a 
renewed rate shock. 
We hold a strong overweight in gold, which performed well 
in both rising- and stable-yield environments and delivered the 
highest return of any asset when high yields were stable. Our 
fixed-income allocation is neutral . Although today’s yields 
offer attractive income, bonds have historically been 
vulnerable when yields were rising and underperformed when 
yield were stable. 
Diversification beyond a traditional 60/40 portfolio 
remains important  at this inflection point. Hedge funds 
generated positive returns across all regimes  - rising, stable 
and falling high -yield periods  - making them a useful 
complement when the direction of rates and the path between 
healthy and unhealthy regimes is uncertain.

### Page 10

Global Market Outlook 10

Key themes

Core scenario (soft landing, 45% probability): We have slightly reduced the probability of a soft landing from 50% by raising 
the odds of a ‘no -landing’. The global economy has  picked up steam this summer, powered by AI investments in the US and 
Asia. This is reflected in robust upgrades to corporate earnings estimates  and PMIs. Fiscal easing in Germany and Japan is 
supporting growth. Meanwhile, inflation has picked up amid higher energy prices due to a resurgen t Middle East conflict. As a 
result, the Fed turned hawkish, hiking rates for the first time since 2023. It was joined by the ECB and BoJ – the first time all 
three major central banks hiked rates in the same month. We expect the Fed to hike twice more, the ECB once more and the 
BoJ thrice more by June 2027. Nevertheless, disinflation should return by next year as energy and tariff effects fade, enabling 
the Fed and the ECB to cut rates by Q3 2027. Meanwhile, China is likely to accelerate fiscal spending to revive domestic growth.  
Upside risk (no landing, 30% probability): We raised the probability from 25% amid an acceleration in the AI-driven investment 
growth. If oil prices ease, US tax cuts, an AI -fuelled stock market boom and fiscal easing in Germany and Japan could boost 
“animal spirits” further. A global defence spending boom, led by the US and Europe, could also spur global growth. 
Downside risk (25% probability): This tail risk scenario includes 15% chance of a stagflation unfolding if geopolitical conflicts 
worsen and oil stays above USD 120/bbl. We also assign a 10% chance to a recession, potentially caused by a more rapid pace 
of rate hikes to quell inflation, a stock market downturn negating the wealth effect, or a bond selloff due to fiscal concerns.

Policy rates watch  
Fed turns hawkish to build credibility as oil reignites 
inflation; likely to hike twice more by June 2027. The US 
economy appears resilient, with consensus estimates for 
2026 GDP clustering around 2.1% for both 2026 and 2027. 
The main engine is the AI investment boom and hyperscaler 
datacentre buildout, reinforced by easy financial conditions 
and healthy private-sector balance sheets. The robust outlook 
was reflected in August’s PMI data, with both manufacturing 
and service sector confidence beating expectations. 
The labour market has picked up  this year , with average 
monthly payrolls of 80 ,000 vs. 10 ,000 in 2025 . The 
unemployment rate at 4.1%  suggests full employment, and 
the workweek lengthened in August. Offsetting vulnerabilities 
include a low savings rate, fading fiscal support, soft real  
personal spending and oil prices close to USD 100/bbl. 
Inflation remains a challenge. Core PCE inflation has run 
above 3% every month since March, and the hawkish case 
rests on supercore inflation remaining around 3 %, raising 
concerns about energy cost pass-through. Nevertheless, 
August's firm inflation print came from airfares, hotels and a 
record jump in wireless plans  (which is idiosyncratic) , while 
shelter cooled and core goods rose just 0. 7% y/y. Core 
consumer inflation at 2. 4% y/y is the lowest since 2021, and 
BEA methodology changes in end-September should 
mechanically shave 0.2-0.3pp off historical core PCE inflation. 
Following the unanimous Fed vote to hike in September, and 
still elevated inflation, we pencil in one more rate hike this 
year, followed by another 25bps hike to 4.5% by June 2027. 
This pace is slower than market pricing of another c. 90bps of 
hikes in the next 12 months. However, we expect disinflation 
to resume next year as the impact of oil prices and tariffs fade. 
This should create space for the Fed to cut rates by Q3 2027.

6 Macro overview – at a glance

Rajat Bhattacharya 
Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 11

Global Market Outlook 11 
ECB to hike once more: Euro area activity has proved more 
resilient than expected. The consensus 2026 growth estimate 
has been revised up to 0.9%, from 0.5% as recently as July, 
with ECB staff projections upgraded to  1.4% for 2027 and 
1.5% for 2028. Manufacturing PMIs have improved to 52.7 
regionally and services PMI to 53.0, helped by German fiscal 
delivery and falling household savings rates. A softer Q3 looks 
weather-driven, due to heatwaves and low Rhine water levels, 
though higher energy prices and real yields still cap demand. 
Headline inflation hit a near three -year high of 3.3% y/y in 
August and may peak around 3.4 -3.6% in Q4 2026 before 
easing through 2027. Underlying pressures look calmer: core 
inflation drifted down to 2.4%, services eased, and negotiated 
wage growth slowed to 2.4% y/y in Q2, down from a peak of 
5.6% in 2024 . Long -term inflation expectations remain 
anchored with no evidence of second -round effects. Inflation 
risks are two -sided – oil above USD 100/bbl and gas above 
EUR 70/MWh are fuelling near -term inflation, which in turn 
should hurt demand, driving inflation below 2% by H2 2027. 
The ECB raised its deposit rate to 2.50% in September, the 
top of neutral estimates. We see scope for one more rate hike 
to 2.75% by year -end before a pause in H1 2027. As such 
pricing of 95bps more tightening toward an above-3% terminal 
rate appears too hawkish. We expect easing of energy-driven 
price pressures to allow for ECB rate cuts by Q3 2027. 
China to boost fiscal spending to support growth. China 
is entering a stabilisation phase in which production is firming, 
but the recovery remains narrow. The official manufacturing 
PMI rose to 49.8 in August from 49.2, with new orders, export 
orders and production back in expansionary territory, while 
export growth accelerated to 25.0% y/y. Momentum, however, 
is concentrated in high -tech and export manufacturing: retail 
sales, fixed-asset investment and credit growth remain soft, 
and the property downturn has yet to bottom. Full -year 2026 
growth should land near 4.5 –4.6%, broadly in line with the 
official 4.5–5% target, as domestic demand stays weak. 
Reflation is under way, but shallow and cost-driven rather than 
demand-led. Headline consumer inflation edged up to 0.8% 
y/y in August and producer inflation to 3.8%, lifted by higher 
commodity and energy prices and by official efforts to curb 
excessive price-cutting. Full-year inflation is expected around 
0.8–1.0%, against roughly zero in 2025, with producer 
inflation swinging from −2.6% to about +2.5%. The persistent 
supply-demand imbalance should cap the reflation impulse. 
Policy settings remain supportive, but with no rush to 
stimulate. Fiscal policy takes the primary role via faster 
spending and utilisation of bond proceeds, with broad fiscal 
spending potentially accelerating in H2 versus a sharp 
contraction in H1, and the augmented fiscal deficit widening 
modestly. Monetary policy plays a complementary role: a 
25bps cut in bank reserve requirements is likely, while the 
policy rate should stay unchanged. Slow public financing 
raises the likelihood of further policy fine-tuning this autumn. 
BoJ to hike once a quarter until Q2 2027. Japan's economy 
is proving more resilient than expected. The consensus 2026 
growth estimate has been revised up over the past month to 
0.8% from 0.6%. Exports rose 19% y/y in August, a twelfth 
consecutive y/y growth, while core machinery and machine 
tool orders indicate higher borrowing costs have yet to dent 
corporate investment appetite. Real wages climbed 2.4% y/y 
in July, the strongest since May 2021. The weak link is the 
household sector: spending continued to contract y/y, leaving 
an unusual two -speed economy in which external and 
corporate demand carry growth while domestic demand lags. 
Underlying inflation sits close to the 2% target, with pressure 
building upstream rather than from demand. Energy and 
commodity prices have risen sharply and earlier yen 
weakness has amplified import costs, with producer inflation 
running well ahead of CPI . The 10-year breakeven inflation 
has risen close to May’s record high of 2.3% amid growing risk 
of pass-through from producer to consumer prices.  
Bank of Japan Governor Ueda, after raising rates by 25bps to 
1.25% in September, alluded to rising inflation pressures, 
stating the policy environment has shifted to a “new phase”. 
He added that the BoJ must now act to prevent inflation from 
overshooting, rather than simply trying to reach the 2% target. 
September’s BoJ decision came in a 7-2 split vote, with two 
members nominated by Prime Minister Takaichi calling for a 
hold. However, Ueda’s post -decision comments lead us to 
believe that the BoJ board will be comfortable with a 25bps 
rate hike every quarter until Q2 2027, taking the rate to 2%.

### Page 12

Global Market Outlook 12

Our view

Foundation: Fixed income assets are a  core holding (Neutral) in our portfolios. Within the asset class, we maintain an 
Overweight stance on EM USD government bonds and an Underweight stance on DM government bonds.  
The Fed’s decision to hike rates in the past week confirms our expectations of a central bank willing to establish its independence 
against political pressure. The Fed's moderately hawkish pivot is helping restore its inflation-fighting credentials. We expect Fed 
to hike twice, one by year-end and another hike in H1 2027 to  bring the Fed funds rate to 4.5%. The price reaction in the US 
government bond yield curve le ads us to believe the short -to-middle part of the curve ha s factored in most of the rate 
expectations. We now prefer to add positions in the 3-7-year bond maturity bucket. As oil price moderates in H2 2027, we see 
room for the Fed to contemplate rate cuts. That would also enable 10-year yields to come down, alongside the front end of the 
curve. Meanwhile, solid corporate fundamentals and supportive technicals should keep yield premiums tight, even if the scope 
for further compression is limited. In EM, we continue to favour EM USD over local currency (LCY) government bonds. 
Opportunistic ideas: We are bullish on European bank additional tier-1 (AT1) bonds (contingent convertibles [CoCos1]; FX-
hedged), US TIPS, AAA-rated CLOs, US utilities corporate hybrids, broad US HY and AUD corporate bonds.

DM rates – Underweight 
We are Underweight DM government bonds. In the US, the 
Fed's policy pivot this month followed Chair Warsh's hawkish 
Jackson Hole speech and recent economic data prints, 
including a pick-up in job creation and inflation over the summer 
and a sharp rebound in oil prices due to the re-escalation of the 
Middle East conflict. Given the unanimous Fed decision to hike 
rates this week, we expect two more hikes by June 2027 on the 
back of robust growth amid strong AI investments and a 
balanced labour market, keeping inflation elevated.  
Over the next few months, we expect the 2-, 10- and 30-year 
US government bond yields to remain rangebound, with the 
10-year yield trading in the 5.00-5.25% range. The reasons 
for this include: 1) oil prices staying elevated and potentially 
rising further due to the fluid geopolitical situation in the Middle 
East; 2) AI capex plans showing no signs of slowing down, 
which continues to drive above -trend growth in the economy 
and AI -related heavy bond issuance. While short -covering 
trades could move bond yields lower temporarily as net-short 
positions in US government bond futures have reached 
extreme levels, we think such downshifts would be short-lived. 
By H2 2027, we expect oil prices to moderate and the 
inflationary effects from tariffs to fade, allowing the Fed to 
contemplate rate cuts. US 30 -year yields, on the other hand, 
are expected to remain elevated as the US fiscal deficit 
remains substantial next year. In addition, we do not think the 
so-called ‘Bessent put’  - expanding purchases of long -dated 
bonds, while skewing new issuance towards shorter 
maturities - will be effective as this does not address the 
fundamental issues  of rising debt levels . These factors are 
likely to keep the 'term premium' elevated. 
In the Euro area and Japan, the ECB and BoJ also hiked rates 
this month. It is the first time on record all three - Fed, ECB 
and BoJ - have hiked in the same month, as they respond 
primarily to a resurgence in energy -driven inflation amid tight 
job markets. BoJ is expected to continue hiking once every 
quarter till mid -2027 as we believe the bank  is behind the 
curve and will need to speed up the pace of rate hikes.

Global Market Outlook 13 
DM corporates – Core holding  
We maintain a Neutral allocation to both DM IG and DM HY 
corporate bonds, with a tactical bias to add exposure on 
episodic weakness, as carry continues to dominate total 
returns across developed market credit. Yield premiums have 
bounced but remain close to historical lows, while yields are 
still attractive, reinforcing a carry-led stance. This tight spread 
regime is anchored by robust issuer fundamentals, strong 
profitability, and resilient balance sheets, despite a visible 
moderation in rating upgrades over the past quarter.  
Fig. 16 AI issuance challenges IG technical backdrop 
AI issuance (total & share of total), US IG gross issuance

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
Technical supply dynamics are reshaping sector dispersion, 
particularly in the IG space, where heavy issuance related to 
AI continues to weigh on the long end of the curve. AI -driven 
supply is approaching USD 220bn for 2026  in US IG  and 
accounting for an increasingly significant share of gross 
issuance and crowding out other long-dated maturity bonds. 
We maintain a preference for selectivity at the long end, 
favouring the front to belly of the curve ( 3-7 years maturity) 
where risk -adjusted carry remains most durable. Issuance -
driven concessions in sectors with strong capital expenditure 
cycles (notably technology, capital goods, and basic 
industries) present opportunities for investors who can 
stomach near term volatility, especially when fund flows have 
remained robust: so far this year, high grade funds have seen 
cumulative inflows of ~8% of AUM. 
Fig. 17 DM IG spreads have bounced lately, but remain 
near historical lows 
Bloomberg Global Aggregate Corp Index, option-adjusted 
spread (OAS)

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
As an income proposition, DM HY continues to be well 
supported, with all-in yields in the mid -7% range and default 
rate still below trend for US HY in 2026. The shorter duration 
profile of HY, combined with its lower sensitivity to term 
premium and rate volatility, makes it an attractive sleeve in the 
current phase of the cycle. Despite experiencing net outflow 
YTD, HY demand has recovered from a net outflow position 
in the past 1-2 months, underscoring broad investor appetite 
for carry strategies even as spreads remain tight. We remain 
attentive to evolving rating migration trends and sector -
specific supply pressures, particularly as upgrades slow and 
new issuance clusters around key theme s. However, we  
continue to see HY bonds as core holding allocations driven 
by healthy issuer fundamentals and stable credit metrics. 
Fig. 18 DM HY spreads are near historical lows as well 
Bloomberg Global HY Aggregate Corporate Index, OAS

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
EM government bonds – Overweight USD, 
core holding for local currency (LCY) bonds 
We re tain an  Overweight position in EM USD government 
bonds and retain EM LCY government bonds as a Core 
holding, reflecting a balanced perspective across both 
regional performance and currency dynamics. In EM USD 
sovereign debt, periods of spread widening have been brief  
and shallow, with attractive carry drawing sustained demand 
amid resilient global growth and stable external conditions. 
Issuance patterns remain front -loaded across many regions, 
reinforcing supportive technicals; relatively light investor 
positioning con tributes to contained risk of unwinding and 
limits market impact during episodes of moderate outflow. 
Regional nuances are increasingly notable. Latin America’s 
sovereign debt has tended to outperform, supported by more 
proactive monetary policy responses and firmer external 
balances. In Asia, higher -rated issuers offer valuable buffer 
during bouts of glob al rate volatility, while select frontier 
exposures within Africa and EMEA remain more sensitive to 
liquidity shifts and changes in risk appetite. Index composition 
also shapes resilience: the EM USD benchmark is less 
exposed to oil price shocks as non -oil importers represent a 
large share of exposure compared to the LCY universe. These 
features are particularly important as spreads across EM USD 
sovereigns hover near historically tight levels, leaving limited 
room for error. We expect returns to be primarily driven by 
carry rather than further spread compression.

Global Market Outlook 15

Our view

We are Overweight global equities as we expect solid earnings growth to drive the market higher . The wave of central 
bank rate hikes in September represent a near-term headwind to equity valuations, but these have been largely priced into 
markets by the rise in bond yields year-to-date. We expect earnings growth to offset valuation headwinds. We are Overweight 
US and AxJ equities, where AI adoption continues to drive growth in the technology and adjacent sectors. 
Within AxJ, we remain Overweight Taiwan, with good visibility on AI-driven growth. We lower China to a Core holding, 
as the valuation re-rating potential appears delayed. Within AxJ, we maintain core allocations to South Korea and India, and we 
are Underweight on ASEAN equities. 
We maintain a Core allocation to Japan, as nominal growth supports broad earnings revision, and a Core allocation to Europe 
ex-UK, which is enjoying an earnings inflexion. We remain Underweight UK equities, which lacks exposure to growth sectors.

8 Equity – at a glance

Sundeep Gantori, CFA, CAIA 
Chief Investment Officer, Equities 
Fook Hien Yap 
Senior Investment Strategist 
Cindy Lam, CFA 
Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 16

Global Market Outlook 16

Expansion at a solid pace  
With economic activity expanding at a solid pace , we expect 
earnings growth to fundamentally drive global equities higher. 
Heading into 2027, rising AI adoption continues to drive 
strong growth  in the technology sector. Recent debates 
about regulating the pace of model developments could 
influence the mix of AI spending, with differing impacts on 
various parts of the AI value chain. However, rising adoption 
and growth in AI use cases should support massive growth in 
capex, benefitting the semiconductor industry, cloud service 
providers and internet platforms. The capex benefits also 
spillover to other industries providing the power infrastructure, 
materials and financing of the investmen ts. The risks are a 
slowdown in AI capex or net job losses from AI disruption, but 
we do not see these having a significant impact yet. 
Rate hikes  from central banks represent a headwind for 
equity valuations, although this has been largely priced in by 
bond markets, given the rise in yields we have seen this year. 
Further rate hikes are being priced by the market. However, 
we expect these headwinds to be offset by solid earnings 
growth in global equities: at 34% in 2026 and 16% in 2027.  
Fig. 21 Global equities continue to be supported by 
strong earnings growth, led by the technology sector  
Earnings growth by sectors for MSCI AC World index

Source: Company reports, Standard Chartered  
US equities – Overweight  
US equity valuations have softened this year  as strong 
earnings growth have outpaced the rise in the market. The 
12m forward P/E of around 20x remains at a premium to other 
markets but justified, in our view, by the high returns on equity, 
differences in sector composition and superior growth outlook. 
In the run-up to US mid-term elections in November, we would 
not be surprised to see higher volatility, though we would use 
any undue corrections as opportunities to add exposure. 
AxJ equities – Overweight 
AxJ enjoys the strongest earnings growth among major 
markets, as its technology sector remains a key 
beneficiary of the AI capex cycle. AxJ also has the lowest 
P/E valuation, given the earnings surge ha s not yet been  
reflected in the market. Within AxJ, we are Overweight 
Taiwan, which benefits from clear visibility on AI -driven 
growth and dominance in manufacturing advanced AI chips. 
We lower China to a Core holding as the valuation re -
rating potential now appears delayed. Share placements for 
AI spending will likely continue, diluting earnings. Regulatory 
concerns still linger from US restrictions on AI chips and 
distillation accusations, even as the Trump-Xi summit signals 
a mutual desire for steady ties. Looming offshore trust taxes 
may lead to further sell down pressures , while domestic 
demand remains weak. However, valuation s remain 
inexpensive with rapid tech innovation and AI -localisation 
investments. Policy stimulus could provide upside. 
South Korea equities remain a Core holding. We are 
positive on the long-term agreements giving greater earnings 
visibility on memory chip s. Positioning is also cleaner, with 
reduced foreign investor exposure and declin ing leveraged 
ETFs. India remains a Core holding amid positive earnings 
revision and strong domestic demand , alongside  macro 
headwinds from RBI rate hikes. We remain Underweight on 
ASEAN, as oil shocks and a supported USD could weigh on 
the region, although Singapore offers defensive growth. 
Fig. 22 Global equity valuations have declined this year, 
with market gains led by earnings growth 
Composition of returns YTD for regional MSCI equity indices

Source: FactSet, Standard Chartered. Min-Max over last 10 years 
Japan equities – Core allocation 
Rising nominal economic growth is supporting solid 
broad-based earnings revisions , with rising rates 
supportive of bank earnings. The fiscal support for strategic 
industries is also pro-growth, offsetting energy shocks.  
Europe ex-UK equities – Core allocation  
Europe ex-UK is seeing an earnings inflection  after three 
stagnant years, with rising rates supportive of banks’ 
earnings. Fiscal expansion is benefitting domestic demand 
and the industrial sector, although earnings growth in the 
region still lags that of the US and AxJ.  
UK equities – Underweight  
The UK lacks exposure to growth sectors, with relatively 
muted earnings growth. We expect this to weigh on the UK , 
making it lag the performance of other markets.
